## IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

The project aims to ensure the distribution of the educational services to households in the target countries, both at the national and regional levels by guaranteeing the availability of the required resources for the implementation of the project. The main physical activities of the project that may have severe or significant adverse environmental and social impacts include: improving the quality of teaching, and schools; including solar PV systems to improve energy efficiency in schools; building new classrooms and school facilities; and establishing community-based environmental education programs. The Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) is designed to address the risks of the project. The ESMP includes measures to avoid or minimize adverse impacts on biodiversity, water resources, soil, equipment and assets prior to implementation works and will be monitored with regular updates. Systems for monitoring the ESMP will also be established to ensure that the environmental and social safeguards are implemented in accordance with the requirements of the GCF Environmental and Social Safeguards Policy (ESGP) and national regulations. The project will also provide a framework for a clear challenge and disaster preparedness and response plan.

### 4.2 Sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH)

The revised GCF Environmental and Social Safeguards Policy (ESGP) includes measures to address SEAH risks. This policy requires that all projects funded by the GCF implement measures to prevent and respond to SEAH. The project will be designed in accordance with this policy for SEAH safeguards.

## Gender policy

The AI provided a gender assessment and action plan with the broader proposed and implemented activities. The gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) component was integrated in the project design from the beginning. The GESI component was designed to ensure that all beneficiaries, including women, children, people with disabilities, and other marginalized groups, have equal access to services and benefits. The project also aimed to promote gender equality and social inclusion through various activities such as training, awareness raising, and advocacy.

The AI developed a strategy for gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) to mainstream gender and social inclusion throughout the BRACE project. During annual review meetings, the GESI component was reviewed and updated based on feedback from beneficiaries and other stakeholders. The project also included specific activities to promote gender equality and social inclusion, such as training on gender-sensitive agriculture practices, providing access to credit for women farmers, and promoting women's leadership in community organizations.

# Green Climate Fund

## 4.3 Risks

### a.i.i Overall project/programme assessment (medium risk)

- The project aims to establish climate-resilient agricultural systems in two LDCs, South Sudan and Ethiopia. These countries are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts such as flooding, droughts, and extreme events like windstorms.
  - **Mitigation:** The project will implement a range of measures to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from agriculture, including improved livestock management techniques.

### b.i.i Accredited entity processing capacity (medium risk)

- The AF has an extensive track record and global experience in implementing climate change mitigation projects. However, the AF's capacity to process and manage multiple projects simultaneously may be a challenge.
  - **Mitigation:** The AF will implement mitigation measures to reduce GHG emissions.

### c.i.i Potential/implementation-specific assessment risks (medium risk)

- Potential stability concerns involving land tenure, governance challenges, and institutional capacity issues could affect the project's implementation. These include:
  - Land tenure insecurity
  - Weak governance structures
  - Limited institutional capacity

### d.i.i Compliance risk (medium risk)

- The project presents moderate compliance risks. Procurement and financial management are key areas of concern.
  - **Mitigation:** The AF will implement measures to address these risks, including:
    - Strengthening procurement processes
    - Enhancing financial management systems
    - Ensuring transparency and accountability

- A multi-country approach is a foreign risk to mitigate the risks leveraging not only financing but also technical assistance. The project will be implemented in two countries with different institutional capacities.
  - **Mitigation:** The AF will implement measures to address these risks, including:
    - Strengthening procurement processes
    - Enhancing financial management systems
    - Ensuring transparency and accountability

- A multi-country approach is a foreign risk to mitigate the risks leveraging not only financing but also technical assistance. The project will be implemented in two countries with different institutional capacities.
  - **Mitigation:** The AF will implement measures to address these risks, including:
    - Strengthening procurement processes
    - Enhancing financial management systems
    - Ensuring transparency and accountability

## Fiduciary

### a.
SCA is the AI for this project. The EEs are SCA, Save the Children International (SCI), Ministry of Education, Youth and Sport (MoEYS).

The MoEYS, SCA, SCI, Global Affairs Canada (GAC), and country government representatives will be involved in the development and implementation of the project. The AI will be responsible for overall project management, including financial oversight, and will distribute funds to implementing partners, based on agreed work plans.

### b.
SCA will not engage in any commercial activities or joint ventures with private sector entities. SCA will utilize the services of local and international consultants to ensure the use of funds and achieving compliance with GCP fiduciary standards. External audits will be conducted annually by an independent auditor approved by SCA.

### c.
SCA will maintain oversight of the project implementation at national and sub-national levels, including through regular field visits. The AI will be responsible for ensuring that GCP funds are properly spent, and risk management in Cambodia, South Asia, and East Africa is implemented. SCA will ensure the use of funds for project implementation, including fiduciary responsibilities in government systems, and will work with partners to ensure compliance with GCP standards. The AI will also ensure that the project is aligned with the objectives of the Culture Organ, and other relevant entities.

## Results monitoring and reporting

### a.
This is an adaptation project that will contribute to 3 of the 6 GCP adaptation results areas (1.2, 4.1, and 5.1). The project will be implemented in Cambodia and South Asia (India, Bangladesh, and Nepal), and in East Africa (Kenya, Ethiopia, and Uganda).

The project is expected to contribute to the following GCP results:

- **Area 1.2**: Increase access to quality education for children in conflict-affected contexts.
- **Area 4.1**: Improve the capacity of national systems to deliver quality education services.
- **Area 5.1**: Strengthen the capacity of national systems to deliver quality education services.

The project will be implemented in Cambodia and Tanzania in the Asia region, and in South Sudan in the Africa region.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP274

## Proposed name
Building the Climate Resilience of Children and Communities Through the Education Sector (BRACE)

## Accredited entity
Save the Children Australia

## Countries
Cambodia, Solomon Islands, Tonga

## Project size
Small

### I. Assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### 1.1 Overview

1. The submission of this funding proposal for the multi-country “Building the Climate Resilience of Children and Communities Through the Education Sector (BRACE)” under the Green Climate Fund (GCF) standard proposal process, for a small-size project, is welcomed.
2. BRACE will focus on strengthening climate resilience in the education sector of three Small Island Developing States (SIDS): Cambodia, Solomon Islands, and Tonga.
3. The BRACE small-scale was conceived during a climate finance dialogue at the margins of the 10th Asia Pacific Ministerial Conference on Disaster Risk Reduction (APMCDRR) held in July 2018 in Ulaanbaatar, Mongolia. The dialogue was co-organized by Save the Children, the GCF and the United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNISDR).
4. BRACE will focus on strengthening climate resilience in the education sector of three Small Island Developing States (SIDS): Cambodia, Solomon Islands, and Tonga.
5. The project aims to contribute to increasing preparedness, changing risk-informed patterns, new level best; and ensure events such as floods, cyclones, droughts, and other hazards do not disrupt children’s learning, health, nutrition, safety, and well-being.
6. BRACE will focus on strengthening climate resilience in the education sector of three Small Island Developing States (SIDS): Cambodia, Solomon Islands, and Tonga.

The assessment of the Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) is based on the funding proposal submitted on 18 April 2019 (version 3). The ITAP’s comments were finalized on 7 May 2019. This assessment was prepared by the ITAP Secretariat.

# School Completion (from Climate and non-climate drivers) via Direct (Children’s) interventions and supporting measures

School completion (from Climate and non-climate drivers) via Direct (Children’s) interventions and supporting measures include the following of children dropping out of school, persistent absenteeism, and disengagement. The interventions are designed to address the root causes of these issues by providing targeted support to students who are at risk of disengaging from school. These interventions can include a range of strategies such as mentoring, tutoring, and counseling.

The leading providers in this area include educational systems, by governments, non-governmental organizations (NGOs), and community-based organizations. The interventions are designed to address the root causes of these issues by providing targeted support to students who are at risk of disengaging from school. These interventions can include a range of strategies such as mentoring, tutoring, and counseling.

## Compartment 1: Building climate-resilient school infrastructure and systems will enhance children’s resilience

### Sub-compartment 1.1: Enhancing school safety and preparedness
Enhance school safety and preparedness; strengthen school identity and community; and enable climate-resilient school infrastructure and systems.

### Sub-compartment 1.2: Enhancing access to climate finance for education
Enhance access to climate finance for education with respect to the support (climate risk informed decision-making).

## Compartment 2: Contribution and knowledge-sharing across schools and education will enhance children’s resilience

School contributions include the following partnerships:

- **Sub-compartment 2.1:** The Australian Government has committed $46.7 million over four years from 2018–19 to support the implementation of the Australian Strategy for Children and Young People (ASYC) and the National Framework for Protecting Australia’s Children 2009-2020.
- **Sub-compartment 2.2:** The Australian Government has committed $46.7 million over four years from 2018–19 to support the implementation of the Australian Strategy for Children and Young People (ASYC) and the National Framework for Protecting Australia’s Children 2009-2020.
- **Sub-compartment 2.3:** The Australian Government has committed $46.7 million over four years from 2018–19 to support the implementation of the Australian Strategy for Children and Young People (ASYC) and the National Framework for Protecting Australia’s Children 2009-2020.

## Impact potential

### Overcoming BRAC exposure to climate-related challenges in children’s education
BRAC exposes children to climate-related challenges. In children’s education, BRAC provides support to overcome these challenges by providing targeted interventions that address the root causes of these issues. These interventions can include a range of strategies such as mentoring, tutoring, and counseling.

### Country context: The AI (Artificial Intelligence) project
The AI project is the independent Technical Advisory Board (TAB) that BRAC draws from CHILD’s network of 10 countries that was launched in 2018. The TAB provides technical assistance to BRAC’s country offices and supports the implementation of the AI project.

# and vulnerability using three climate risk indices and the Human Development Index (HDI)

BRACE further identified the three prioritizing countries from across different geographies and income levels. The BRACE framework is designed to support the development of country-specific climate change adaptation strategies and to inform the design of regional and global climate risk assessment efforts by successfully demonstrating the process of integrating climate change into the national planning cycle. This approach has been validated through a series of pilot studies in several countries, including the United Kingdom, Germany, and the Philippines.

## Selection of countries

The selection of countries for this analysis was based on three criteria: (1) the need to include countries from different regions; (2) the need to include countries with varying levels of vulnerability to climate change; and (3) the need to include countries that have already begun to integrate climate risk into their national planning processes. The selection process involved a review of existing climate risk assessments, including global, regional, and country-specific assessments, as well as an analysis of the availability of data and information on climate risks and vulnerabilities in each country.

### Criteria for selection

The selection criteria were designed to ensure that the countries selected represented a diverse range of regions, income levels, and vulnerability to climate change. The following criteria were used:

1. **Regional representation**: The selection process aimed to include countries from different regions around the world.
2. **Vulnerability to climate change**: The selection process also considered the level of vulnerability to climate change in each country.
3. **Data availability and information on climate risks and vulnerabilities**: The selection process took into account the availability of data and information on climate risks and vulnerabilities in each country.

### Climate risk assessments

The BRACE framework builds on existing climate risk assessments, including global, regional, and country-specific assessments. These assessments have been used to inform the development of the BRACE framework and to support the integration of climate risk into national planning processes. The BRACE framework also incorporates lessons learned from other initiatives that have successfully integrated climate risk into national planning processes.

### Climate vulnerability indices

The BRACE framework uses three climate vulnerability indices to assess the level of vulnerability to climate change in each country: the Climate Vulnerability Index (CVI), the Climate Risk Index (CRI), and the Human Development Index (HDI). These indices are used to identify countries that are most vulnerable to climate change and to inform the development of country-specific adaptation strategies.

## Benefits of the BSC project

The benefits of the BSC project include:

- Most vulnerable people and communities (50 per cent of GCF contributions).
- Small island developing states (SIDS) (50 per cent of GCF contributions).
- Least developed countries and small island developing states (LDCs/SIDS) (50 per cent of GCF contributions).

The project anticipates reaching 67,938 direct beneficiaries, and 14,644 indirect beneficiaries. The project will also reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 210 tons of CO2 equivalent annually through the use of renewable energy sources.

# The benefits include increased safety of children, enhanced personnel and congruity of lead around schools to climate-related impacts (Requirements 2.12 and 1.4). Students, school personnel, and community members are more likely to be informed regarding the early warning and disaster communication systems (output indicators 3.1.1 and 3.1.2). This is achieved through the integration of climate change education into the curriculum since Requirement 3.1.3.

## BRACE project

The BRACE project aims to enhance the resilience of schools, communities, and staff by the BRACE project. This includes identifying areas vulnerable to climate-related hazards, developing school disaster risk management plans, and providing training on disaster preparedness. The project also seeks to reduce school dropouts to 20%<sup>2</sup>, which highlights the severe impact of climate-related hazards on education. It is expected that the BRACE project will be implemented by climate-related disasters in 2019 with benefits being seen in the next five years.

### Output indicators

The BRACE project aims to achieve the following output indicators:

- **Output indicator 3.1.1**: Reduce school dropouts to 20%<sup>2</sup>.
- **Output indicator 3.1.2**: Increase awareness of climate-related hazards among students, teachers, and community members.
- **Output indicator 3.1.3**: Integrate climate change education into the curriculum.

The BRACE project will also focus on demonstrating the benefits of climate resilience to schools, communities, and staff by providing training and support. The project will be implemented in collaboration with local governments and other stakeholders.

### IAFP

The IAFP also recommends that the project aims to place adequate safeguards to ensure that the project does not exacerbate existing inequalities or create new ones. This includes ensuring that the benefits of the project are equitably distributed among all participants, including those who may be most vulnerable to climate-related impacts.

## Paradigm shift potential

### Scale: Medium

The BRACE project demonstrates the presence for education is a demonstration of building human capital, economic and environmental resilience via impact on reducing the operation of schools, communities, and staff. The project aims to enhance the capacity of schools to adapt to climate change by providing training and support to school personnel.

#### Goal 1: Achieve a paradigm shift by building climate-resilient school systems

Goal 1 aims to achieve a paradigm shift by building climate-resilient school systems that can withstand the impacts of climate change. This includes enhancing the capacity of schools to adapt to climate change by providing training and support to school personnel.

# climate change natural and educational levels in the three targeted countries

This is the second report from a project on integrating climate change into pre-service and in-service teacher education programmes at universities and colleges of education in Ghana, Kenya and Uganda. The first report was published in October 2019. This report focuses on the integration of climate change issues into the curricula of these institutions and the development of teaching materials to support this process. It also describes the activities undertaken by the project team to promote the integration of climate change issues into teacher education programmes.

## The ALL IN project

The ALL IN project aims to integrate climate change issues into pre-service and in-service teacher education programmes at universities and colleges of education in Ghana, Kenya and Uganda. The project is funded by the European Union's Erasmus+ programme and is being implemented by a consortium of universities and colleges of education in the three countries.

### Objectives

The objectives of the ALL IN project are:

- To integrate climate change issues into pre-service and in-service teacher education programmes at universities and colleges of education in Ghana, Kenya and Uganda.
- To develop teaching materials to support the integration of climate change issues into teacher education programmes.
- To promote the use of these teaching materials by teachers in their classrooms.

### Activities

The activities undertaken by the project team include:

- Conducting a needs assessment to identify the specific needs of each country with regard to the integration of climate change issues into teacher education programmes.
- Developing a framework for the integration of climate change issues into teacher education programmes.
- Developing teaching materials to support the integration of climate change issues into teacher education programmes.
- Piloting the use of these teaching materials by teachers in their classrooms.
- Evaluating the effectiveness of the teaching materials and the integration process.

### Outcomes

The expected outcomes of the ALL IN project are:

- A framework for the integration of climate change issues into pre-service and in-service teacher education programmes at universities and colleges of education in Ghana, Kenya and Uganda.
- Teaching materials to support the integration of climate change issues into teacher education programmes.
- Teachers who have been trained to use these teaching materials effectively in their classrooms.

## Contextual factors

In the view of the IAF, the three countries in the theory of change are considered as the context within which the project will be implemented. The contextual factors that will be relevant to the achievement of the intended outcomes and to evaluating a contribution to the theory of change include:

- The political and economic stability of each country.
- The level of commitment of the government to climate change education.
- The level of support for the integration of climate change issues into teacher education programmes by the ministries of education in each country.
- The level of support for the project by other non-governmental drivers (or highly trusted) organisations.

## The extent to which dry education schools are effectively adapted and can deliver climate change education

The extent to which dry education schools are effectively adapted and can deliver climate change education is a critical factor in the success of the project. This includes the development of appropriate teaching materials, the provision of training for teachers, and the establishment of partnerships with other organisations that can support the delivery of climate change education.

### Scoping

The number of schools involved in the project will be determined by the type of activities across schools in each country, and the proportion of drivers and indirect beneficiaries significantly impacted. The project will involve a minimum of 30 schools per country (15 pre-service and 15 in-service) to ensure that the project has a significant impact on the delivery of climate change education in each country.

## The TAF considers that the planned development of a longer-term financing strategy...

### The TAF considers that the planned development of a longer-term financing strategy...

The TAF considers that the planned development of a longer-term financing strategy, integrated under one component, will be critical to help search funders for rotating and scaling up their investments. The TAF also notes that the current financial model may not be sustainable in the long term. The TAF recommends that wider sources of financing and programming are also explored.

# BRIECE Benefits to Gender Equity and Social Inclusion

BRIECE is designed to deliver three core benefits to gender equity and social inclusion, recognition, and disproportionately low participation, although the funding period has not yet been announced. The project will be implemented in two phases: a baseline survey and a needs assessment, followed by interventions to address these issues.

The first benefit is to increase women's participation in decision-making processes at all levels. This includes promoting gender equality and social inclusion through targeted interventions that aim to empower women and marginalized groups. The second benefit is to improve the livelihoods of smallholder farmers, particularly women, by providing access to separate soil fertility, water conservation, and sustainable land management practices. The third benefit is to increase the participation of women in local governance and decision-making processes.

The Gender Assessment and Action Plan (GAAP) clearly sets out the core benefits of BRIECE, which are not limited to gender equity but also include social inclusion and recognition of marginalized groups. These benefits will be delivered through interventions that address the needs of vulnerable populations, including women, children, and people with disabilities.

## Goal 2: Environment

The co-benefits will directly benefit through activities that contribute to gender equality and social inclusion in environmental management. This includes promoting climate-smart agriculture, improving water efficiency, and reducing greenhouse gas emissions. The project will also support the development of sustainable livelihoods for women and marginalized groups by providing access to credit, training, and market opportunities.

## Goal 3: Increased Healthy Food Production (local)

# Green Paper on Children

## Reference information on the perceived value of engagement by children and their families in such matters

### Needs of the recipient

1. All new participation councils have multiple experienced challenges that common to all districts family by the different sector. Children, in order to ensure that they are able to participate effectively in the decision-making process, need to be supported with a range of resources including training, support and guidance on how to engage with children and young people.

2. The New Zealand Family Violence Clearinghouse (NZFVC) has developed a series of guidelines for engaging with children and young people. These guidelines provide practical advice on how to engage with children and young people in the context of family violence. They are designed to support practitioners in their work with children and young people, and to help them to better understand and respond to the needs of this population.

3. The guidelines highlight the importance of child-centered approaches and the need for practitioners to be aware of the unique challenges that children and young people face when engaging with family violence services. They also emphasize the importance of cultural competency and the need for practitioners to be aware of the cultural factors that may impact on a child's experience of family violence.

4. The guidelines provide practical advice on how to engage with children and young people, including tips on how to create a safe and supportive environment, how to communicate effectively with children and young people, and how to support them in their participation in decision-making processes.

5. Overall, the ITFP assesses the needs of the recipient as high.

# Green Impact Campaign

## Introduction to the Green Impact Campaign

The Green Impact Campaign is an initiative that aims to educate and empower students and community members about climate change impacts and solutions. The campaign focuses on providing resources and opportunities for individuals to take action in their communities.

### Overview of the Green Impact Campaign

The Green Impact Campaign (GIC) is a program designed to engage students and community members in addressing climate change. The GIC provides educational materials, workshops, and other resources to help participants understand the impacts of climate change and how they can take action.

### Partnerships and Schoolholder Engagement

The GIC has established partnerships with various organizations and institutions to promote its mission. These partnerships include:

- **US Department of Energy**: Provides funding and support for the GIC.
- **National Renewable Energy Laboratory (NREL)**: Supports the development of educational materials and resources.
- **Environmental Protection Agency (EPA)**: Assists in promoting the GIC's message and providing technical assistance.

### Impact of the Green Impact Campaign

The GIC has had a significant impact on communities across the United States. Through its partnerships and educational programs, the GIC has helped to raise awareness about climate change and provided resources for individuals to take action. The GIC has also been recognized for its innovative approach to education and community engagement.

### Future Directions

As the GIC continues to grow and expand, it will focus on building new partnerships and developing new educational materials. The GIC will also continue to work with existing partners to promote its mission and provide resources for individuals to take action against climate change.

# Developmental benefits in the longer term

Table 1 of the economic and financial analysis (Lemma 34) emphasises the variety of probable benefits for climate adaptation and for the environment. The most important of these are:

- **Economic benefits**: These include the creation of jobs, the development of new skills, and the transfer of technology.
- **Environmental benefits**: These include the reduction in greenhouse gas emissions.

## The education sector

The education sector will benefit from climate change in a sub-national context. Component 1: The improvement of school buildings under input 12 could lead to the creation of jobs for local workers and the development of new skills. The use of photovoltaic systems to improve access to renewable energy, lighting and internet connectivity (input 3) will also create jobs and develop new skills in the installation and maintenance of these systems. The upgrading of driving water systems, handwashing stations and latrines (input 6) will also benefit local workers and WASH businesses in their communities.

The IAF will support the development of climate-proof schools in the context of the IAP. The IAP would fund GCF funding channel component 1 to improve the resilience of BRACI interventions. ## II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

i. If approved by the Board, this would be the first GCF climate project in the adaptation portfolio that is implemented through a multi-country approach. This could offer opportunities to expand climate adaptation into national educational systems and to build capacity for climate change adaptation. It is important that all countries are willing to promote and validate this change. As BRACE is planning the approach in three countries, it is critical to ensure that the project is designed to be scalable and replicable across other countries of the region and offers the potential for wide learning, scaling, and replicability, and to achieve long-term impacts.

ii. The ITAP also proposes recommendations to the AF as follows:

iii. Pay special attention to safeguards to ensure that school infrastructure upgrades achieve their intended outcomes and do not inadvertently increase vulnerability to climate change risks (paragraph 27).

iv. Provide flexibility in planning that allows for adaptation to local contexts, including the use of locally available materials and labor (paragraph 31 above).

v. Develop a fully costed plan that aligns with project objectives and is based on a thorough understanding of the costs associated with school upgrades.

vi. Detail the overall M&E approach, with proper baselines, so that it is fit for purpose to track progress towards achieving the project’s objectives. This should include indicators for climate-related education outcomes (see paragraph 78 above) and be aligned with the GCF M&E framework (paragraph 15 above).

vii. The ITAP recommends that the Board approve the funding proposal.

# Overall results from the Independent Technical Advisory Panel

We note with shock and sadness the recommendation to prepare exit strategies for the three countries with clear evidence of a decline in the risk of their project efforts falling over time.

## Recommendations from the ITAP

- We have reviewed the following reports to our recommendations.
- Put in place adjusted safeguards
  - **Adjusted safeguards**
    - Adjusted safeguards are not required and will continue practicing in public good news updates which include the use of a risk register. This includes working with our regional monitoring team to ensure that we are on track this, including within our regular monitoring.
- Develop a financial sustainability plan
  - Thank you for the recommendation. As you highlight these, this will be a key part of our work in the coming months. We have already started developing a financial sustainability plan and will continue to do so. Finance models developed and implemented in Congopeel 1 and the Congopeel 2 project are being reviewed by the ITAP. The Ministry of Education are evaluated under SRACE to continue this work in the future.
- Develop a fully costed O&M plan
  - We agree for the need of a O&M plan for the school that will receive support from the government. We have already started developing a financial sustainability plan and will continue to do so. Finance models developed and implemented in Congopeel 1 and the Congopeel 2 project are being reviewed by the ITAP. The Ministry of Education are evaluated under SRACE to continue this work in the future.
- Detail the overall M&E approach
  - All countries should develop a detailed M&E plan that will be used during the next M&E cycle and beyond. This includes developing an M&E framework, indicators, data collection tools, and analysis methods. We are fully committed to preparing better for each country's monitoring and evaluation Technical Advisory Committee, accompanied by the Ministry of Education. We have already started working on this approach.

# Introduction

The Resilience Resource of Children and Communities through the Education Sector (BRACE) project aims to improve the resilience capacities and climate resilience of children and communities in the Philippines. This is achieved by empowering schools to become centers of learning and community engagement on disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation. The project is implemented by UNDP, with funding from the Green Climate Fund (GCF), and is being executed through the Department of Education (DepEd) as the education sector lead agency.

BRACE will be implemented in five provinces: Albay, Bohol, Eastern Samar, Leyte, and Palawan. These provinces were selected based on their vulnerability to climate change impacts such as typhoons, floods, and droughts. The project aims to enhance the capacities of schools and communities to adapt to climate change through improved access to climate information, early warning systems, and disaster risk management.

BRACE will be implemented in three components:

- **Component 1: Enhancing Access to Climate Finance for Education**, with outputs and outcomes as outlined in Outputs 1.1 and 1.2.
- **Component 2: Enhancing Access to Climate Finance for Education**, with outputs and outcomes as outlined in Outputs 2.1 and 2.2.
- **Component 3: Communication and Knowledge Sharing on Climate and Education**, with outputs and outcomes as outlined in Outputs 3.1.

## For the BRACE Project, please see Appendix A.

### Component 1: Enhancing Access to Climate Finance for Education

The BRACE project will be implemented through a series of activities that include:

- **Domain 1: Laws, Policies, and Institutions**
    - Output 1.1: Review and update existing laws, policies, and institutions related to climate change.
    - Output 1.2: Develop and implement new laws, policies, and institutions related to climate change.

- **Domain 2: Participatory Leadership and Decision-Making**
    - Output 2.1: Conduct participatory leadership training for school administrators and teachers.
    - Output 2.2: Establish a network of schools committed to participatory decision-making on climate change.

- **Domain 3: Safety, Duty, and Risk**
    - Output 3.1: Develop and implement safety protocols for schools in disaster-prone areas.
    - Output 3.2: Conduct risk assessments for schools and communities.

### Component 2: Enhancing Access to Climate Finance for Education

The BRACE project will be implemented through a series of activities that include:

- **Domain 4: Laws, Policies, and Institutions**
    - Output 4.1: Review and update existing laws, policies, and institutions related to climate finance.
    - Output 4.2: Develop and implement new laws, policies, and institutions related to climate finance.

- **Domain 5: Participatory Leadership and Decision-Making**
    - Output 5.1: Conduct participatory leadership training for school administrators and teachers.
    - Output 5.2: Establish a network of schools committed to participatory decision-making on climate finance.

### Component 3: Communication and Knowledge Sharing on Climate and Education

The BRACE project will be implemented through a series of activities that include:

- **Domain 6: Safety, Duty, and Risk**
    - Output 6.1: Develop and implement safety protocols for schools in disaster-prone areas.
    - Output 6.2: Conduct risk assessments for schools and communities.

## Based on the experiences gained from the BRACE project, the project aims to include lessons learned and best practices into existing policies and programs. This will further enhance the capacity of the Department of Education to promote gender equality and empower women in all that they do.

# Methodology

1. The gender mainstreaming and action plan were prepared by the feasible teams located at central, South Sudan and Tongale. The Gender Equality Policy was developed in collaboration with the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) Gender Equality Policy, the Government of South Sudan Ministry of Gender, Child and Social Welfare (MoGCSW), and the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA). The plan uses a gender equality lens to analyze data and activities concerning gender issues. Where necessary, the development and implementation of the Gender Action Plan were guided by the Gender Equality Policy.

2. **Data Collection**
   - Primary data were collected in South Sudan and Tongale.
   - Focus group discussions (FGDs) were conducted with 100 participants from the Government of South Sudan Ministries of Education and Gender, parents, teachers, and students.
   - Secondary data were collected from the Ministry of Education and the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA).
   - Data collection was carried out between October 2023 and June 2024. These datasets were coded by the UNDP Gender Equality Policy team in collaboration with the Government of South Sudan.
   - The data were analyzed using SPSS software for a comprehensive understanding of the key differences among the different groups.

3. **Secondary Data**
   - Analysis was conducted on the three countries and at a global level for Compartment 2 and 3.
   - The data were collected from the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA) and the Ministry of Education.
   - The data were analyzed using SPSS Software.
   - The data were collected from the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA).

4. **Gender Action Plan Implementation**
   - The gender action plan was developed in collaboration with the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA).
   - The implementation of the Gender Equality Policy was guided by the United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA) and the Ministry of Education.
   - The data were analyzed using SPSS Software.

# Global Findings on Gender Equality and Social Inclusion and Climate Change

Gender equality is inextricably linked to sustainable development goals such as gender equity, social inclusion, climate change adaptation, disaster risk reduction, and resilience. Women are shown to be at a higher risk than men in the face of natural disasters due to their lower socio-economic status.

## Gender Equality and Social Inclusion

Gender equality and social inclusion (GESI) is particularly relevant when addressing issues such as poverty, health, education, and employment. It is more likely to be achieved if there is greater attention paid to gender and other marginalized groups. These inequalities are likely to increase what men are and are not able to do.

### Gender and Disaster Risk Reduction

Gender and disaster risk reduction (G-DRR) is a gender and age-based approach that aims to reduce the risks of disasters by addressing the different needs, capacities, and responsibilities of women, girls, boys, and men. This includes understanding the different roles and responsibilities of each group in disaster management.

### Climate Change Adaptation

Climate change adaptation (CCA) refers to adjustments in natural biological systems and human social systems in response to actual or expected climate stimuli or their effects, which are the results of the climate system interacting with the social system. This includes changes in land use, water resources, and infrastructure.

### Gender and Climate Change

Gender and climate change (GCC) is a field that focuses on the impacts of climate change on women and men differently. It aims to ensure that gender equality is considered in all aspects of climate change adaptation and mitigation policies and programs. This includes addressing issues such as access to resources, decision-making power, and vulnerability.

### Gender and Social Exclusion

Gender and social exclusion (GSE) refers to the process by which individuals or groups are excluded from full participation in society due to their gender. This can include barriers to education, employment, and political participation.

## Evidence

Evidence shows a direct relationship between gender equality and climate change adaptation. For example, women who have access to land and resources are more likely to be able to adapt to the impacts of climate change. Additionally, women who have access to education and training are more likely to be able to take action to mitigate the impacts of climate change.

### Gender Equality Index

The Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI) is a framework for measuring gender equality across four dimensions: economic participation and opportunity, educational attainment, health and survival, and political empowerment. The GGGI shows that while there has been progress in some areas, there is still a significant gap between men and women.

### Climate Change Adaptation Index

The Climate Change Adaptation Index (CCAI) is a measure of a country's ability to adapt to the impacts of climate change. The CCAI takes into account factors such as vulnerability, exposure, and capacity to cope with climate change. The CCAI shows that while there has been progress in some areas, there is still a significant gap between developed and developing countries.

### Sources

- United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)
- World Bank
- International Union for Conservation of Nature (IUCN)
- Global Gender Gap Report 2021
- Climate Change Adaptation Index 2021

#### Child, sex and forced marriage and violence (CEFM)

A new analysis by Save the Children indicates that around 1 in 5 girls globally are married as children. New analyses by Save the Children indicate that CEFM is a global problem affecting every year and half around five million girls. The number of girls at risk of CEFM has increased with higher levels of education, poverty, conflict and displacement. The rate of CEFM was by 11% in 2021 compared to 2021. The analysis also indicates that the prevalence of CEFM is highest among girls who are out of school. The practice of a girl being married, having in poverty, and living in rural areas increases the risk of CEFM. Girls who have been married at an early age are more likely to drop out of school and have long-term impacts on the family unit. Girls who are married at an early age are also more likely to be exposed to violence and abuse from their husbands and families. # Country Level Findings on Gender Equality and Social Inclusion

## Key Findings

### Gender

Gender inequality affects persons who are poor, women, children, and people with disabilities. Female employment in agriculture as a share of total female employment has increased from 45% to 53%. Women's participation in decision-making processes is low due to gender norms and stereotypes. Women are more likely than men to be engaged in unpaid care work, which limits their time for paid labor.

### Social Inclusion

Social exclusion affects persons who are poor, women, children, and people with disabilities. Family employment in agriculture as a share of total female employment has increased from 45% to 53%. Women's participation in decision-making processes is low due to gender norms and stereotypes. Women are more likely than men to be engaged in unpaid care work, which limits their time for paid labor.

### South Sudan

In South Sudan, women, men, boys, and girls experience different socio-economic opportunities and constraints based on their age, sex, location, marital status, and disability. The impact of conflict and displacement has exacerbated gender inequalities. Women's participation in decision-making processes is low due to gender norms and stereotypes.

Women are more likely than men to be engaged in unpaid care work, which limits their time for paid labor. Women's access to land, credit, and markets is limited by cultural norms and practices. Women's participation in the labor force is lower than that of men, and they are more likely to be employed in low-paid and informal jobs.

Women's access to education and health services is limited by cultural norms and practices. Women's participation in decision-making processes is low due to gender norms and stereotypes.

# Tanganyika

The history of Tanganyika is a story of pioneering game reserves and improving women's lives through literacy. This is part of her enduring legacy of conservation and development, which has been passed down from generation to generation.

This includes the many years she spent educating the community and working with the Queen and Prince Philip to establish the 300-kilometre-long corridor that links the Serengeti National Park in Tanzania with the Ngorongoro Conservation Area. This corridor is now a vital part of the wildlife migration route.

# Key Data

## Demographic Data

### Indicator | Cambodia | South Sudan | Tonga

#### Population
- **Cambodia**: 16,842,211 (July 2020 est.)
  - Total population is approximately 15.7 million with around 80% living in rural areas.
  - 39.47% of the total population are under 25 years old.

- **South Sudan**: 11.19 million (July 2020 est.)
  - Total population is approximately 11.1 million with around 80% living in rural areas.
  - Children under the age of 15 constitute 43.6% of the total population, while people aged 65 and over make up 3.1%.

- **Tonga**: 107,290 (July 2020 est.)
  - Total population is approximately 107,290 with around 80% living in rural areas.
  - The population of the Kingdom of Tonga is heavily influenced by the population leading country Tongan and Niuean.

#### Age
- **Cambodia**: Children under the age of 15 constitute 36.4% of the total population, while people aged 65 and over make up 7.2%.

- **South Sudan**: South Sudan has a youthful population, with 50.9% of its population being under the age of 15. The median age is approximately 18 years.
  - Approximately 43.6% of the population is under the age of 15.

- **Tonga**: Based on Tonga's 2021 census, nearly half (49%) of the population is aged between 25 and 44 years old, with a median age of approximately 30 years.
  - The population of Tonga is heavily influenced by the population leading country Tongan and Niuean.

#### Gender balance
- **Cambodia**: Of the total population, women and girls make up 51.1%.

- **South Sudan**: South Sudan's population has a slight male majority with approximately 52% of the population being men.
  - Approximately 43.6% of the population is under the age of 15.

- **Tonga**: Tonga's population leans slightly female, with 51.3% being female and 48.7% male.

#### Head of household
- **Cambodia**: 76% of 2.9 million households are headed by a man.
  - Approximately 30% of the total population is under the age of 15.

- **South Sudan**: Approximately 50% of households are headed by women, with men heading approximately 48%.

- **Tonga**: In Tonga, 26.1% of households were headed by a woman.

## Marginalized groups

### LGBTQI+ people in Cambodia

- LGBTQI+ people in Cambodia face numerous challenges, more than 90% faced heavy oppression. 96% experienced discrimination.
- Discrimination is often based on sexual orientation and gender identity.
- The LGBTQI+ community faces multiple barriers to accessing health services.

### People with disabilities (PWDs)

- PWDs also experience discrimination, children from low socioeconomic households and children from other minorities have multiple barriers to accessing education and healthcare.
- 20% of PWDs in Cambodia are illiterate. 35% of PWDs live below the poverty line.
- 61% reported that they were denied access to services because of their disability.

### Indigenous Peoples

- 22 Indigenous Peoples were identified in Cambodia comprising 1.34% of the total population.
- 80% of Indigenous Peoples are illiterate, 95% live below the poverty line.
- According to the 2019 Cambodia Labour Force Survey findings, the estimated population of Indigenous Peoples is around 670,000 people.

### South Sudan

#### Challenges in education

- South Sudan faces significant challenges in addressing the needs of marginalized groups.
- Discrimination and lack of access to education and healthcare are often reported by indigenous peoples to education and healthcare, often due to discrimination and lack of accessible services.
- Internally displaced persons (IDPs) face additional barriers to accessing education.

#### Education for PWDs

- PWDs in South Sudan face significant challenges in accessing education.
- 64% of PWDs have never attended school. Only 10% completed primary school, and only 2% completed secondary school.
- The LGBTQI+ community faces social exclusion, discrimination, and lack of access to services.

### Tonga

#### Gender stereotypes

- Gender stereotypes are much more widely accepted in Tonga than elsewhere in the Pacific region.
- Women face significant barriers to accessing education and healthcare. Women who have always been at the core of Tongan society are now facing increased challenges due to gender stereotypes.
- LGBTQI+ children can become victims of violence, discrimination, and exclusion.

## Poverty: Economic Empowerment and Labor

### Operation in Cambodia

- **Population**: The population in Cambodia is multilingual with over 90% speaking Khmer.
- **Poverty Rate**: 18.9% of the population lives below the poverty line, which is considered moderate poverty.

### Labor Market Participation Rates

- **Men and Women**: The labor market participation rate for men is around 75%, while it is lower for women due to family responsibilities.
- **Youth Employment**: Youth employment is a significant issue with unemployment rates of over 15 years being 20.4% in South Sudan.

### Industry and Labor

- **Industry**: The largest industry in Cambodia is the garment manufacturing sector, employing around 600,000 people.
- **Labor Laws**: The minimum wage for workers in the garment industry is $170 per month.
- **Child Labor**: There are reports of child labor in the garment industry, with children as young as 5 working long hours.

### Social Protection

- **National Social Protection Council (NSPC)**: Established in 2017 to develop social protection policies and programs for vulnerable groups.
- **Social Safety Nets**: The NSPC aims to provide social safety nets for the elderly, persons with disabilities, and other vulnerable individuals. Elderly and persons with disabilities are entitled to an ID card that allows them access to various services.

### South Sudan

- **Unemployment Rate**: Over 15 years is 20.4% in South Sudan.
- **Agriculture**: The majority of the population depends on agriculture for their livelihood, but poor infrastructure and lack of investment hinder growth.
- **Youth Unemployment**: Youth unemployment is a significant issue, with many young people struggling to find jobs.

### Conclusion

- **Economic Challenges**: Both Cambodia and South Sudan face economic challenges that impact labor markets and social protection policies.
- **Government Efforts**: Governments in both countries are working on improving economic conditions and providing better social safety nets for their citizens.

# Large-scale land occupations in South Sudan have further exacerbated land security issues in the region

Large-scale land occupations in South Sudan have further exacerbated land security issues in the region. Over half of the total land area in South Sudan has been allocated to foreign investors for agricultural or resource extraction purposes, with an estimated 5 million hectares of land having been leased out to foreign companies. This displacement has led to over 4 million people being displaced from their ancestral lands.

The large-scale land acquisitions have also contributed to rising tensions, liver and social unrest. The displacement of local communities has led to increased competition for access to basic resources such as water and food. This has further exacerbated the already fragile security situation in South Sudan. Foreign investors have been accused of violating local laws and customs, and of failing to respect traditional land tenure systems.

## Impact on Local Communities

The impact on local communities has been significant. Over 50% of the total land area in South Sudan has been allocated to foreign investors for agricultural or resource extraction purposes. This displacement has led to over 4 million people being displaced from their ancestral lands, contributing to rising tensions and social unrest.

### Foreign Investment in South Sudan

Foreign investment in South Sudan has been a contentious issue. While some argue that it brings much-needed economic development and job creation, others point to the negative impacts on local communities and the environment. The displacement of local communities has led to increased competition for access to basic resources such as water and food.

## Climate Change and Disaster Risk Management

Climate change is a significant challenge in South Sudan. The country is highly vulnerable to natural disasters such as floods, droughts, and landslides. These events have been exacerbated by climate change, leading to increased displacement and food insecurity. The government has implemented various measures to address these challenges, including the establishment of early warning systems and the development of climate-resilient infrastructure.

### Climate Change Mitigation

The government has also taken steps to mitigate the impacts of climate change. This includes the implementation of renewable energy projects and the promotion of sustainable agriculture practices. These efforts are aimed at reducing greenhouse gas emissions and promoting sustainable development.

## Conclusion

In conclusion, large-scale land occupations in South Sudan have further exacerbated land security issues in the region. The displacement of local communities has led to increased competition for access to basic resources such as water and food, contributing to rising tensions and social unrest. Climate change is a significant challenge in South Sudan, leading to increased displacement and food insecurity.

- [2] World Bank. "Land Degradation and Restoration in South Sudan." Available at [link](https://www.worldbank.org/en/country/southsudan/brief/land-degradation-and-restoration).

#### Tonga

The National Analysis of Tropical Cyclone Gita in the Kingdom of Tonga identified heightened vulnerabilities for specific populations, including individuals with disabilities, its elderly population, women, and children. The analysis also highlighted the vulnerability of households, single mothers (especially those with many dependents), and people with disabilities. These groups are particularly vulnerable to natural disasters such as a mudslide or tsunami.

Women's livelihoods in Tonga are heavily reliant on natural resources, such as a mudslide or tsunami. This vulnerability is exacerbated by gender-based disparities in aid distribution. Female-headed households are more likely to be affected by these events and often require additional support from their families and communities. Women, who are often required to be the primary caregivers for children and elderly family members, are frequently disenfranchised by aid providers, who often require the women to be present to receive assistance.

### Gender components of national policies

#### The Constitution of Cambodia (1993)

The Constitution of Cambodia guarantees equality before the law and provides equal protection to all citizens regardless of their race, color, sex, religion, political opinion, or any other status. It also prohibits discrimination based on gender.

#### Development policies

Cambodia has mainstreamed gender equality into its national development strategies plans and reforms.

- The Royal Government of Cambodia’s National Strategic Development Plan 2019-2023 outlines the government's commitment to promoting gender equality.
- The National Policy on Women in Development (NPWD) was adopted in 2008, which aims to promote women's empowerment as part of the Cambodian government's strategy for growth, employment, and poverty reduction.

#### Ministry of Women’s Affairs

Cambodia established gender equality through the establishment of the:

- Ministry of Women’s Affairs (MoWA) in 1997.
- National Committee on Women’s Affairs in 2004.
- Technical Working Group on Gender in 2004.
- Running Monitoring Clubs in 2008 across the nation, and many others.

# Climate policies

Canada has ratified the UN Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). In 1998, Canada established a National Climate Change Committee with a mandate to provide advice and recommendations on climate change policy and programs in response to climate change.

## Climate Change Act

In March 2021, Canada committed to develop a **10-year Canada-wide Action Plan** to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 40-45% below 2005 levels by 2030. The plan will include measures to address climate change through increasing women's participation in climate engineering, making gender equality funding available, and ensuring that the response is responsive to the needs of women and girls. The plan will also include measures to ensure that the response is responsive to the needs of women and girls.

### Canada's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC)

Canada's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) acknowledges the importance of gender equality in addressing climate change. The NDC includes a commitment to reduce greenhouse gas emissions by 30% below 2005 levels by 2030.

### Sustainable Development

The National Climate Change Committee is part of Canada's National Council for Sustainable Development. The committee is responsible for providing advice and recommendations on sustainable development issues in Canada. The committee also provides advice to the government on climate change policies and programs.

Conducting a national committee for disease management (NCDM) in 1998 with a structure at both national and subnational levels.

### South Sudan

The Government of South Sudan has committed to international treaties aimed at promoting gender equality and women's empowerment. These include the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Maputo Protocol on the Rights of Women in Africa.

The Ministry of Gender, Child, and Social Welfare (MoGCSW), which plays a key role in implementing these international commitments, including the National Gender Policy (2013) and the Sexual and Reproductive Health Policy (2016). These policies aim to promote gender-based violence and women's empowerment.

#### Gender commitment of national documents

The Transitional Constitution of South Sudan (2011)

The Constitution guarantees equal rights and protection of women's rights. Article 18 states that "all persons are equal before the law and are entitled to equal protection of the law." Article 34 provides for the elimination of discrimination against women and girls, including in employment, education, and health care.

Article 51 is the Constitutional mandate that "women shall have the right to participate fully in public life and have equal rights with men."

**Page 177**

# National Gender Policy (2013)

The National Gender Policy serves as the primary framework guiding gender equality and women’s empowerment in South Sudan. It aims to promote women’s participation in education, health, agriculture, and governance, and seeks to protect women's rights and enhance their role in society. The policy also addresses issues such as early marriage, female genital mutilation, and other harmful practices that prevent women from realizing their full potential.

# Sexual and Gender-Based Violence (SGBV) Bill 2016

The SGBV Bill focuses on addressing violence against women and girls in both public and private spheres. It includes provisions to prevent, punish, and protect victims of GBV. The bill also emphasizes the importance of providing support services for survivors, including legal aid, medical treatment, and psychological support.

# National Action Plan on Women, Peace, and Security (2015-2020)

The National Action Plan on Women, Peace, and Security aims to enhance women’s participation in peacebuilding and post-conflict reconstruction efforts. It addresses the disproportionate impact of conflict on women and girls and promotes their inclusion in decision-making processes.

# South Sudan’s Government Engagement Strategy and Action Plan (2015-2030)

The strategy focuses on enhancing women's engagement in political, economic, and social spheres. It includes initiatives to promote women's participation in leadership positions, improve access to education and healthcare, and address gender-based violence.

# Education Sector Development Strategy Plan (2017-2026)

The Education Sector Development Plan focuses on increasing girls' enrollment and retention rates. It aims to eliminate barriers that prevent girls from accessing quality education by addressing issues such as early marriage, child labor, and gender-based barriers that prevent girls from attending school.

# National Girl’s Education Strategy 2014-2038 for South Sudan

This strategy aims to create a comprehensive framework to address the persistent challenges hindering girls' access to education in South Sudan. It emphasizes the transformational impact of quality education on girls and women, aiming to empower them economically and socially. The strategy also seeks to create an enabling environment that supports girls’ educational development.

# Disability and Social Inclusion Lines and Policies

## National Disability and Inclusion Policy (2019)

Although focused on persons with disabilities, this policy includes a gender lens to ensure that women and girls with disabilities are not left behind. It aims to empower women with disabilities from birth and pregnancy through old age.

## Second National LGBTIQ+ Communities Policy

This policy recognises the experiences of LGBTIQ+ people and their families, and acknowledges the need for inclusive policies that address discrimination and promote equality. It also highlights the importance of a whole-of-government approach to support LGBTIQ+ communities.

# Climate Change Lines and Policies

## National Climate Change Policy (2016)

South Sudan’s National Climate Change Policy integrates gender considerations alongside past women and children’s disproportionate effects on climate change. The policy aims to empower women and girls, promote their participation in decision-making processes, and enhance their resilience to climate change.

## The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) (2018)

This was developed in response to the growing impacts of climate change, with a focus on gender considerations. The NAP aims to address immediate and long-term climate risks and vulnerabilities. It integrates gender considerations into national adaptation planning processes under the Paris Agreement. It recognises gender considerations as essential for effective climate change adaptation.

# Targeted Strategy Development Framework 2019-2023

This policy is one component of the UK Government’s approach to tackling serious and organised crime (SOC) and promoting community safety. It sets out the government's approach to targeted strategy development and provides a framework for local partnerships in all areas of the country.

## Voluntary National Review 2019

The Voluntary National Review 2019 provides an overview of progress made on the Targeted Strategy Development Framework over the course of the review period.

During the review process, the government identified 69 key SDGs indicators that are most relevant to its development priorities. One such indicator is SDG 5.4, which tracks the proportion of women in decision-making positions in local governments. This highlights the government's focus on equality and women’s empowerment.

### UK Convention on the Elimination of all Forms of Discrimination against Women

The UK has signed the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), with gains in participation and inclusion. However, challenges remain, especially in public participation and representation.

### Domestic Violence

The government has implemented a multi-faceted approach to address domestic violence. The strategy includes targeted interventions for victims, offenders, and perpetrators. This is supported by the [Ending Violence Against Women and Girls Strategy](https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/ending-violence-against-women-and-girls-strategy) launched in 2016.

The government has also introduced the Domestic Abuse Act 2017, which includes a range of measures to support victims. The strategy is supported by targeted interventions and community-based approaches.

#### Key Initiatives

- **Family Project Legal Aid Grants in 2018**: A financial grant for legal aid space for children in the Domestic Violence Act.
- **SafeLives Campaign**: Launched in 2019 to support victims of domestic violence.
- **National Domestic Violence Helpline**: Operated by Refuge and Women's Aid.

### Key Objectives

- Support local partnerships to address community safety issues.
- Promote equality and diversity within the criminal justice system.
- Enhance public participation in community safety initiatives.

# The Women’s Affairs and Gender Equality Division within the Ministry of Internal Affairs and Communications (MIC)

The Women’s Affairs and Gender Equality Division within the Ministry of Internal Affairs and Communications (MIC) promotes gender equality policies and measures related to ICT (Information and Communication Technology). It also engages in activities such as promoting women's participation in science and technology, and supporting women who are "returning a child is raised for three years" to return to work. The division also conducts research on the status of women in various fields.

The Act on Promotion of Women’s Participation and Advancement in the Field of Science and Technology (2015) establishes domestic violence against women, particularly sexual harassment, as a crime. It also provides measures for preventing such crimes. The Act on the Prevention of Harassment Such as Power Harassment and Sexual Harassment (2014) criminalizes domestic violence, sexual harassment, and negligence intended against women.

The division works with the tension between cultural norms and legal restrictions regarding gender equality in Japan.

## Women’s Surrogacy is Illegal

Women's surrogacy is illegal as an accepted form of parenthood. The Criminal Offense Act (1948) prohibits such acts both here and abroad, and clearly penalizes children born through surrogacy.

Similarly, the PFA recognizes domestic violence against women, particularly sexual harassment, as a crime. It also provides measures for preventing such crimes. The division works with the tension between cultural norms and legal restrictions regarding gender equality in Japan.

Specifically, such as the measurement of gender equality of Pacific peoples, particularly women. It also provides a platform for the exchange of information on best practices by member countries. The targets are constructed to address issues such as gender equality through the implementation of the Revised National Policy on Gender Equality (NPGAE). # The Legislative Assembly’s amendments

## Age of Consent

The age of consent for girls is 16, while there's no minimum age of consent for boys.

### Criminal Code (1985)

The Criminal Code (1985) states that a child under the age of 14 cannot give consent to sexual activity. This age applies regardless of whether the activity is consensual or not. The legal age of consent in Canada is 16 years old.

## Disability and Social Inclusion Laws and Policies

### National Policy on Disability Inclusive Development

Toronto’s National Policy on Disability Inclusive Development (2014-2018) promotes a whole-of-government approach, meaning different businesses collaborate to improve the lives of people with disabilities. Key points include:

- Dedicated and permanent seats for disability inclusion.
- Expanded support services for people with disabilities.

### New Education Act

The New Education Act (2013) mandates inclusive education, meaning all children have the right to an equal learning environment. This includes:

- Accessible schools and classrooms.
- Supportive teaching practices.
- Inclusive curriculum and resources.

### Social Inclusion

There are various laws directly impacting social inclusion, like the Health Service Act (2018) and the Employment First Act (2017). These promote equal opportunities for all. The Constitution Act (1982) also includes provisions against discrimination based on sex, sexual orientation, age, and disability.

### United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities

There is a general commitment to the principles of equality and non-discrimination in the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities.

# Domain 2: Social Norms and Roles, Responsibilities and Time Use

## Discrimination

Discriminatory social and gender norms, roles and stereotypes have significant impacts on the lives of women and girls. These norms can both enable and constrain their ability to change and better capacities to adapt. Three socially constructed norms and rules that affect women's empowerment are:

1. **Patriarchy**: This pervasive norm affects many areas of life, including women in Domain 3 & 4.
2. **Masculinity**: This norm is associated with power, income and control over resources.
3. **Gender roles**: These roles are deeply ingrained and affect the division of labour.

### Patriarchy

Patriarchy is a system of patriarchal values and discriminatory gender norms are heavily ingrained. These socially constructed norms have caused great harm and women's subordination to men.

108) Will climate change negatively affect water availability and quality? The poorest people in the world will be most affected by changes in water availability. Water is essential for human health and well-being and is also a key input into agriculture and other economic activities. Climate change will affect water availability through changes in precipitation patterns, increased evaporation and changes in snow and ice melt. This will have significant impacts on water resources and the people who depend on them.

109) How can we ensure that women are empowered to take action on climate change? Women are often at the frontline of climate change impacts and are well-positioned to lead efforts to address these challenges. However, they may face barriers such as lack of access to information, resources and decision-making power. To empower women to take action on climate change, it is important to provide them with the knowledge, skills and resources they need to adapt to the changing climate. This includes providing access to education, training and capacity building opportunities, as well as promoting gender equality and women's empowerment.

South Sudan:
Women in South Sudan face unique challenges due to conflict-related gender-based violence, cultural beliefs, and discriminatory gender norms. These challenges are exacerbated by climate change, which affects their ability to access and use resources such as land and water. Women in South Sudan are also disproportionately affected by climate change, with limited access to education, health care and economic opportunities.

# participation in households

However, the particular customs of South Sudanese society and the associated laws can prevent women's participation in fields, the market, and other economic activities. This is particularly true for married women who are responsible for domestic chores, child care, and the upbringing of children. In addition, the high fertility rate among women is a long-standing practice of economic engagement and possibilities to generate income and employment opportunities for women. However, this has been accompanied by even greater discrimination. When they can participate, they have less decision-making power in the household and are often excluded from economic activities. Customs, traditions, beliefs and practices that discriminate against women are the major causes of gender inequality. The unequal distribution of resources and the lack of access to education and health services are also contributing factors.

## Legal framework of the country

The Constitution of South Sudan provides for equal protection under the law without discrimination on the basis of sex. The government has not effectively enforced these protections, however, and women face significant legal disadvantages. For example, the law does not provide for equal pay for equal work, and women are not entitled to own property or inherit land.

### Women's rights

Women in South Sudan have been denied basic human rights such as freedom of movement, education, employment, and access to health care. The government has not taken any steps to address these issues. In addition, the lack of legal protection for women has led to a rise in domestic violence and sexual harassment.

### Traditional customs and practices

Traditional customs and practices on property joint women's economic power have been a major obstacle to their participation in the economy. These include the practice of polygamy, which is common among many South Sudanese communities. Polygamy is a cultural practice that has been passed down from generation to generation. It is a practice that is deeply rooted in the culture and traditions of the people.

### Women's economic empowerment

Women have been excluded from the labor force due to cultural norms and practices. This has led to a lack of access to education, health care, and other basic services. In addition, women are often denied the right to own property or inherit land. As a result, they are unable to participate in the economy.

### Women's role in agriculture

Women play a significant role in agriculture in South Sudan. They are responsible for planting, harvesting, and processing crops. However, they are often denied access to credit and other financial services. This has led to a lack of investment in agriculture and a decrease in productivity.

## Tonga

Tongan society presents a contrast to many Pacific island societies where women have traditionally been excluded from decision-making processes. The Tongan constitution provides for equal rights for men and women, and the government has taken steps to promote gender equality. However, traditional customs and practices still play a significant role in the lives of Tongan women.

### Women's participation in politics

Women are underrepresented in politics in Tonga. There are no women in the parliament, and only a few women hold positions in the government. This is despite the fact that women make up more than half of the population.

### Women's economic empowerment

Women in Tonga face significant barriers to economic empowerment. They are often denied access to credit and other financial services, and they are less likely to be employed in the formal sector. In addition, women are often responsible for the care of children and the elderly, which can limit their ability to participate in the economy.

### Women's health

Women in Tonga face significant health challenges. They have high rates of maternal mortality, and they are at a higher risk of contracting HIV/AIDS. In addition, women are often denied access to reproductive health services.

### Women's education

Women in Tonga have lower levels of education than men. This is due to cultural norms and practices that discourage girls from attending school. As a result, women are less likely to be employed in the formal sector and are more likely to be engaged in informal employment.
