and an emerging patriarchal influence. Traditional customs and Christian values have, at times, been interpreted to justify a more traditional social hierarchy, creating tension between men and women.

### Like Cambridge and South Sudan

Traditional gender roles often assign men as the primary breadwinners and women as the primary caregivers of children. These roles are reinforced through various means such as:

- **Economic roles**: Men are often responsible for duties such as hunting, farming, and child-rearing.
- **Social roles**: Women are often responsible for duties such as cooking, cleaning, and child-rearing.

These roles can be reinforced by cultural practices that emphasize the importance of gender roles in community gatherings and social processes. Women often play supporting roles in community leadership, providing input on issues affecting women and children. Men may also play a role in community decision-making, especially when it comes to issues affecting the entire community or engaging in traditional ceremonies.

### Male-dominated activities

Male-dominated activities such as hunting, farming, and warfare are often seen as more prestigious than female-dominated activities such as cooking, cleaning, and child-rearing. This can lead to a perception that men are more important than women, which can be reinforced by cultural practices that emphasize the importance of male-dominated activities.

### Gender roles in the workplace

Gender roles can also be reinforced in the workplace, where certain jobs may be seen as more appropriate for men or women. For example, construction and engineering jobs may be seen as more appropriate for men, while nursing and teaching jobs may be seen as more appropriate for women. This can lead to a perception that certain jobs are more valuable than others, which can reinforce traditional gender roles.

### Gender roles in the family

Gender roles can also be reinforced in the family, where certain tasks may be seen as more appropriate for men or women. For example, men may be responsible for providing financial support for the family, while women may be responsible for caring for children and maintaining the home. This can lead to a perception that certain tasks are more valuable than others, which can reinforce traditional gender roles.

### Gender roles in society

Gender roles can also be reinforced in society through various means such as:

- **Media**: Media representations of men and women can reinforce traditional gender roles.
- **Education**: Educational systems can reinforce traditional gender roles by teaching boys and girls different subjects or emphasizing different skills.
- **Religion**: Religious beliefs and practices can reinforce traditional gender roles.

### Gender roles in the workplace

Gender roles can also be reinforced in the workplace through various means such as:

- **Job segregation**: Certain jobs may be seen as more appropriate for men or women, which can lead to job segregation.
- **Wage gaps**: Men may earn more than women for doing the same job, which can reinforce traditional gender roles.

### Gender roles in the family

Gender roles can also be reinforced in the family through various means such as:

- **Division of labor**: Certain tasks may be seen as more appropriate for men or women, which can lead to a division of labor.
- **Power dynamics**: Men may have more power than women in the family, which can reinforce traditional gender roles.

# Genderia

## UK

Women have been found to have less power to voice their opinions and influence decision-making processes in both their homes and in wider society. [10] Further, the lack of women's participation in political life is a major barrier to achieving gender equality. The increasing number of women elected to parliament has not led to an increase in the number of women holding ministerial positions or leading parliamentary committees. In 2005, only 19% of ministers were women, compared with 36% of members of the House of Lords. [11] The number of women in senior positions in political parties has also increased slowly; in 2004, women accounted for 18% of the membership of the Labour Party's National Executive Committee and 15% of the Conservative Party's National Convention. [12]

## Canada

There have been a small increase of women in public leadership over the past decade. In 2006, there were 37 women (24%) in the Canadian House of Commons, compared with 43 (29%) in 2008 and 50 (33%) in 2011. [13] The number of women in the Senate has also increased; in 2006, there were 27 women (31%), compared with 39 (43%) in 2008 and 42 (47%) in 2011. [14]

## United States

Women have made significant gains in political representation over the past decade. In 2006, there were 78 women (17%) in the U.S. House of Representatives and 14 (15%) in the Senate. [15] By 2010, these numbers had increased to 79 (17%) in the House and 17 (19%) in the Senate. [16]

## South Sudan

Women have been involved in peace processes at the global level for decades, but their participation has often been limited to lower-level decision-making positions. In recent years, there has been an increase in women's representation in high-level peace processes. In 2005, the number of women in peace negotiations increased from 13% to 18%. [17] By 2010, this number had increased to 24%. [18]

## Global Trends

The global trend towards greater gender equality has been slow but steady. In 2005, the Inter-Parliamentary Union reported that women accounted for 16% of all members of parliament worldwide. [19] By 2010, this number had increased to 18%. [20]

## Conclusion

While there have been some gains in women's political representation over the past decade, significant barriers remain. Women continue to be underrepresented in decision-making positions and face discrimination and violence in their efforts to participate in political life.

# Strengthening decision-making

## Gender inequality affects all aspects of life

Gender inequality affects all aspects of life. It is a root cause of poverty,
inequality and violence against women. It undermines the progress made in women's
education and in decision-making for, with progress made in other areas.
It also reinforces gender stereotypes that make being the responsibility of men and
women to be responsible for accepting or rejecting decisions.

## Gender equality is a human right

Gender equality is a human right. It is enshrined in the UN Charter and in all core
international human rights treaties, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW). The principle of gender equality is also reflected in the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action, which emerged from the Fourth World Conference on Women held in 1995. Gender equality is also a necessary foundation for a peaceful, prosperous and sustainable world.

## Gender equality is smart economics

Gender equality is smart economics. It drives a more stable and inclusive economy.
It enhances productivity and innovation. It opens up new markets. It is one of the best ways to boost
economic growth and development. The World Economic Forum's Global Gender Gap Report 2018 shows that gender parity can increase global GDP by US$23 trillion, or 17%.

## Gender equality is good for business

Gender equality is good for business. It enhances the performance of companies.
It increases the efficiency of organisations. It improves the bottom line.

## Gender equality is important for social justice and human rights

Gender equality is important for social justice and human rights. It ensures that women and men enjoy equal rights, responsibilities and opportunities in all spheres of life. It is also a necessary condition for the achievement of the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) and other internationally agreed goals.

## Gender equality is a precondition for peace and security

Gender equality is a precondition for peace and security. It helps to prevent conflict and to build peace.
It promotes social cohesion and social justice. It enhances the participation of women in decision-making processes at all levels.

# Excerpts from a report on educational challenges

Attending to secondary school is even lower. This is exacerbated by factors such as long-term detention for school, inadequate sanitation facilities, and gender-based violence.

The situation is worse in rural areas where girls are more likely to be engaged in household chores, farming, and family care. The education of girls and women's issues in society.

## Key challenges

- **School and community awareness**: There is a need to increase awareness on the importance of education for girls.
- **Gender-based violence**: Girls face sexual harassment, rape, and defilement while going to school or on their way back home. This has led to many dropping out of school due to pregnancy, HIV/AIDS, and other sexually transmitted diseases.

## Recommendations

- **Government intervention**: The government should increase the number of girls in secondary schools by providing scholarships.
- **Community involvement**: Communities should be involved in addressing issues affecting girls' education. This can be done through community dialogues and awareness campaigns on the importance of girls' education.
- **Policy implementation**: Policies that support girls' education should be implemented effectively.

## Additional challenges

- **Early marriages**: Early marriages are a major challenge to girls' education, particularly in rural areas where cultural practices favor early marriage over education.
- **Poverty**: Poverty is another significant challenge. Many families cannot afford school fees and other related expenses for their children's education.

## Recommendations continued

- **Economic empowerment**: Programs that empower families economically can help reduce the burden of school fees and other expenses, making it easier for girls to stay in school.
- **Counseling services**: Providing counseling services to girls who have experienced violence or abuse can help them cope with their experiences and continue with their education.

## Specific cases

### South Sudan

In South Sudan, the situation is particularly dire. According to UNICEF, only 25% of schools are functional, and less than 10% of these provide secondary education. The lack of infrastructure and resources makes it difficult for girls to access education.

### Kenya

In Kenya, early marriages and pregnancies are major challenges. A report by the Kenya National Bureau of Statistics indicates that one in every three girls is married before the age of 18. This significantly reduces their chances of completing their education.

## Conclusion

Addressing these challenges requires a multi-faceted approach involving government policies, community engagement, economic empowerment, and support services for affected girls. Only through such comprehensive efforts can we hope to see significant improvements in girls' education.

Based on community grants, gender-focused or economic empowerment programs, petitions, and women’s access to such systems, a trend has emerged: women in South Sudan are increasingly highlighting their less than 25% representation in leadership positions.

The lack of female participation in decision-making processes is attributed to cultural practices that limit women's ability to engage in political activities. This is exacerbated by the ongoing conflict, which has led to increased displacement and limited access to education for women and girls.

The United Nations Population Fund (UNFPA) reports that women’s participation in education is just 10%, citing economic and social challenges. However, there are efforts underway to promote gender equality through education and training programs aimed at empowering women and girls.

The Global Girl Education Strategy (2020-2030) aims to address the educational needs of girls, focusing on improving enrollment, retention, and completion rates for girls in primary and secondary schools. It also emphasizes the importance of safe learning environments and the empowerment of girl education champions.

The government has implemented several policies aimed at promoting women’s economic empowerment and gender equality. These include initiatives such as microfinance programs, business training, and support for women-owned enterprises. However, despite these efforts, women still face significant barriers to full participation in the economy.

To enhance civil society engagement, there are emerging efforts to strengthen gender-responsive budgeting processes at both national and sub-national levels. This includes improving access to financial resources, developing policies, and how to report on gender equality. Women’s rights organizations are also advocating for increased access to Economic Empowerment Funds (EEF) as a means to protect vulnerable populations.

### Tonga

Tongan girls are underrepresented in primary education, with a Gender Parity Index of 0.94 compared to boys’ index of 1.0. This disparity is more pronounced in secondary and tertiary education, where Tongan women still face significant barriers to access and completion.

The Ministry of Education and Training has implemented several initiatives aimed at increasing girls' enrollment and retention rates in schools. These include scholarship programs, mentoring, and support for girls’ educational pursuits. However, despite these efforts, the gender gap remains a challenge.

The Ministry of Education and Training has also developed a Gender Equality Policy to address the challenges faced by Tongan women in education. The policy aims to promote equal access to education for all students, regardless of their gender. It also includes measures to address the social and cultural barriers that prevent girls from accessing education.

Tongan women face significant challenges in accessing higher education due to financial constraints, family responsibilities, and societal expectations. Despite these obstacles, there are increasing numbers of Tongan women pursuing tertiary education, particularly in fields such as nursing, teaching, and business administration.

The government has implemented several programs aimed at promoting gender equality and empowering women. These include initiatives such as microfinance programs, business training, and support for women-owned enterprises. However, despite these efforts, women still face significant barriers to full participation in the economy.

To enhance civil society engagement, there are emerging efforts to strengthen gender-responsive budgeting processes at both national and sub-national levels. This includes improving access to financial resources, developing policies, and how to report on gender equality. Women’s rights organizations are also advocating for increased access to Economic Empowerment Funds (EEF) as a means to protect vulnerable populations.

### References

- Global Girl Education Strategy. (2020-2030).
- Ministry of Education and Training, Tonga. (2020). Gender Equality Policy.
- Economic Empowerment Funds (EEF) Report. (2021).

## Cambodia
Cambodia exhibits some of the highest rates of physical or sexual violence by their current or most recent partner. The prevalence of intimate partner violence (IPV) is 37% for women and 18% for men. This violence affects women and LGBTQ+ people in both private and public spaces, including at home, workplaces, schools, and on the streets. The COVID-19 pandemic has exacerbated gender-based harassment to the workplace, sexual exploitation, and abuse through trafficking and other forms of violence.

According to a UN Population Fund (UNFPA) 2023 Situation Report, "The prevalence of intimate partner violence in Cambodia is among the highest in the world." The report notes that these figures are based on self-reported data from women and men. The report also highlights the need for more research into the causes of gender-based violence and the impact it has on individuals and communities.

Traditionally, many Cambodians rely on their parents to marry. Arranged marriages are common in rural areas where young people may not have access to education or employment opportunities. However, this practice is changing as more young people seek independence and autonomy.

Cambodia is encouraged to get married before having kids. While men and women are generally equal in terms of rights and responsibilities, there are still some cultural barriers that prevent women from fully participating in society.

### Room Bude:
189) Girls as young as 12 are often subjected to marriage for love, a practice that has been going on for centuries in some parts of the world. In South Sudan, a third of girls in the country are pregnant before turning 15. This practice is driven by poverty and lack of education about reproductive health. The consequences are severe, including increased risk of maternal mortality, sexually transmitted infections (STIs), HIV/AIDS, teenage pregnancy, school dropout, and negative health outcomes for both mother and child.

### South Sudan:
190) South Sudan ranks 168th out of 187 countries in the 2015 Global Peace Index. The country has been plagued by conflict since its independence in 2011, with ongoing violence that has led to widespread displacement and humanitarian needs.

### Humanitarian Needs:
191) According to the 2016 Humanitarian Needs and Response Plan, 8.4 million people are in need of humanitarian assistance in South Sudan, including 3.9 million children and 1.5 million pregnant or breastfeeding women. The conflict has led to severe food insecurity, lack of access to healthcare, and limited access to clean water and sanitation.

### United Nations Mission:
192) In 2013, the United Nations Mission in South Sudan documented numerous incidents of sexual violence against civilians, including children. The mission reported that rape was used as a weapon of war by both government forces and opposition groups.

### Education:
193) ERRA is working with UNICEF to establish special protection units at all schools in South Sudan to ensure the safety of students and prevent gender-based violence. These units are designed to provide a safe space for children to report incidents of sexual abuse and receive support and counseling.

### Gender Inequality Index:
194) Further, high levels of gender inequality of opportunity and empowerment persist in South Sudan. The country ranks 172nd out of 188 countries on the United Nations Development Programme's (UNDP) Human Development Index (HDI). This is due to a combination of factors including conflict, poverty, lack of education, and limited access to healthcare.

### Conclusion:
195) In conclusion, South Sudan faces significant challenges in addressing gender inequality and ensuring the rights and well-being of its citizens. The country must work to end the conflict, improve access to education and healthcare, and promote gender equality to achieve sustainable development.

# Recommendations

The recommendations below are based on the findings of the GEI project. We would also expect these to be valuable and relevant beyond the boundaries and implementation of the project.

## Building Climate-Resilient School Infrastructure and Systems

### Domain 1: Laws, Policies and Institutions

**Recommendation 1:**
- Integrate climate change considerations and gender-responsive climate change and education policies and programming into national education sector plans.
- Include climate change in the curriculum at all levels. Support the engagement of women, girls, and youth in climate-related activities.
- Ensure that climate change is integrated into teacher training curricula and teaching materials.
- Incorporate climate change considerations into school safety policies and practices. Support relevant government stakeholders and non-governmental organizations (NGOs) to develop and implement these policies.
- Integrate gender-responsive policies and practices.
- Promote the equal enjoyment of children, youth, women, people with disabilities, diverse ethnic and racial groups and other marginalized communities in climate change education, implementation and monitoring of each policy.

### Domain 2: Social Norms, Roles, Responsibilities and Time Use

**Recommendation 1:**
- Consider GEI when preparing teaching and learning materials for children.
- Ensure that these materials are gender-responsive and include diverse examples of girls and boys, women and men. The materials should also be culturally appropriate and address the needs of marginalized groups. We do not know how to address this and how practical it is.

**Recommendation 2:**
- Recognize children face different barriers in accessing education, especially for those living in areas affected by climate hazards. Though vulnerability is often higher among girls, boys also experience these challenges.
- Include GEI when conducting school safety risk assessments in schools and communities to ensure that all students are safe at school.

**Recommendation 3:**
- Engage and empower local communities to participate in planning, implementing and monitoring of each policy.
- Focus on the integration of gender-responsive education policies (GEPs) into national education systems.

# Gender and Social Inclusion Action Plan

## Component 1: Women's Empowerment and Gender Equality

### Purpose/Objectives
IFAD is committed to promoting gender equality and empowering women as a key element of its overall development objective. This plan outlines IFAD’s strategy for achieving this goal, focusing on the following objectives:
- To global knowledge, policy dialogue and learning.
- To strengthen capacities at all levels.
- To promote gender-responsive policies and practices.

### Component T: Enabling Conditions
Component T focuses on creating an enabling environment to support the implementation of the Gender Equality Action Plan (GEAP). This component is essential for the success of other components as it addresses the root causes of gender inequality. It includes:
- Strengthening systems and processes.
- Enhancing capacities at all levels.

### Output 1.1: Enabling systems and policies are in place and applied
#### Activity 1.1.1: Facilitate preparation for the next GEAP cycle
- **Objective**: To ensure that the next GEAP cycle is well-prepared and informed by lessons learned from the current cycle.
- **Actions**:
  - Conduct a comprehensive review of the current GEAP.
  - Identify best practices and areas for improvement.
  - Develop recommendations for the next GEAP cycle.

#### Activity 1.1.2: Facilitate policy dialogue
- **Objective**: To promote policy dialogue on gender equality issues within IFAD.
- **Actions**:
  - Organize policy dialogues with key stakeholders, including staff from different regions and departments.
  - Ensure that policies are inclusive and responsive to the needs of all genders.

### Results Framework
| Indicator | Baseline (2019) | Target (2023) |
|-----------|-----------------|--------------|
| Gender Equality Index (GEI) | 75.6 | 85.0 |
| Percentage of policies with gender equality components | 40% | 60% |

### Responsibilities
- **Lead**: Gender Equality and Social Inclusion Unit (GESI)
- **Support**: Human Resources Department (HRD)

### Costs
- **Total Budget**: USD 175,320

# Project Actions

## Object 1: Civilian

### GESI Actions, Initiatives and Targets

#### Activity 1.2.2 Enhance knowledge and skills of community leaders and educators to implement climate change adaptation strategies in their communities

**GESI actions:**
- **South Sudan:** Engage with local leaders and religious groups.
- **Kenya:** Work with local leaders to develop action plans.

**Affiliates:**
- **Target:** 100% of professional development programs with gender-responsive modules.
- **Indicator:** 85% of participants report increased knowledge in climate change adaptation strategies.

#### Activity 1.4.2 Strengthen capacity of community members to implement climate change adaptation strategies

**GESI actions:**
- **South Sudan:** Provide leadership training that includes gender equality and climate change adaptation.
- **Kenya:** Train youth and women with disabilities in leadership, advocacy, and environmental conservation.

**Affiliates:**
- **Target:** 80% of participants report increased knowledge in climate change adaptation strategies.
- **Indicator:** 75% of participants report increased knowledge in climate change adaptation strategies.

### Timeline

| Timeframe | GESI Program | USD Education Advocate | USD Climate and DRR Manager |
|----------|---------------|-----------------------|----------------------------|
| Y 1, Q 3  | 100%          | 50%                   | 25%                        |

### Responsibility

- **GESI Program Manager**
- **USD Education Advocate**
- **USD Climate and DRR Manager**

### Costs

- **Y 1:** $1,464,102
- **Y 2:** $1,344,598

# Appendix A: BRACE Theory of Change

## Overview

### Mission
Provide a comprehensive framework for understanding and implementing change.

### Vision
A world where change is predictable, manageable, and beneficial to all stakeholders.

## Components

### Stakeholder Engagement
- Identify key stakeholders.
- Engage through regular communication and feedback loops.
- Ensure representation in decision-making processes.

| Stakeholders | Responsibilities |
|--------------|-------------------|
| Management   | Strategic planning |
| Employees    | Day-to-day operations |
| Customers    | Feedback and satisfaction |
| Community    | Social impact and sustainability |

### Resource Allocation
- Efficiently allocate resources to maximize impact.
- Monitor resource usage and adjust as needed.

| Resources     | Allocation Strategy |
|---------------|---------------------|
| Financial     | Budget planning      |
| Human         | Skill development    |
| Technological | Innovation          |

### Change Management
- Implement change management strategies to minimize disruption.
- Provide training and support for all stakeholders.

| Strategies       | Implementation Steps |
|------------------|----------------------|
| Communication    | Regular updates      |
| Training         | Skill-building sessions|
| Support Systems  | Help desks, mentors   |

### Performance Measurement
- Establish key performance indicators (KPIs).
- Regularly review and adjust KPIs based on feedback.

| KPIs             | Measurement Criteria |
|------------------|----------------------|
| Customer Satisfaction | Surveys              |
| Employee Productivity  | Metrics               |
| Financial Impact     | Revenue reports       |

### Continuous Improvement
- Foster a culture of continuous improvement.
- Regularly review and update the theory of change.

## Conclusion

The BRACE Theory of Change provides a structured approach to managing and implementing change effectively. By focusing on stakeholder engagement, resource allocation, change management, performance measurement, and continuous improvement, organizations can navigate change more successfully.

# Appendix B: Consultations Overview South Sudan

## Context and Objectives

The consultations for the BRAACE project were conducted with the primary aim of gathering comprehensive insights and feedback to inform the project's approach to enhancing the quality of education in South Sudan. The consultations aimed to engage a wide range of stakeholders, including children, parents, teachers, and community leaders, to understand their experiences and perspectives on educational challenges and opportunities.

## Timeline

The consultations spanned six months, from January to June 2024. This period allowed for an extensive engagement process, enabling the project team to gather diverse perspectives and experiences that would inform the project's approach.

## Geographic Scope

The consultations were conducted in four key courts:

1. Bar (Jonglei State)
2. Aweil (Northern Bahr el Ghazal State)
3. Rumbek (Lakes State)
4. Wau (Western Equatoria State)

These locations were selected to represent a range of states and communities for providing diverse insights into the educational landscape in South Sudan. Further consultations with additional stakeholders were selected based on a range of demographic considerations, as well as input from local partners.

## Levels of Engagement

1. **National Level**
   - Consultations included high-level stakeholders, including government officials and representatives.
   - The focus was on aligning the BRAACE project with national policies and strategies.

2. **State Level**
   - Consultations were conducted at state level, engaging State Ministry of Education (SMoE) personnel and local NGOs operating in the states.
   - These consultations identified state-specific challenges and resource allocations to enhance education outcomes.

3. **County Level**
   - County-level consultations involved local government officials, teachers, and community organizations.
   - These meetings aimed to identify county-specific needs and tailor interventions accordingly.

4. **Community Level**


---
**Page 221**
---

Direct engagement with teachers, parents, students, and other community members through FGDs and community meetings.

The project aims to develop a shared understanding of the daily challenges faced by communities and schools, particularly in relation to climate impacts.

### Methods of Engagement

1. **Focus Group Discussions (FGDs)**
   - Conducted with four groups, including parents, community leaders, teachers, and students.
   - Aimed to include participation from women, youth, and marginalized people.
   - Focused on education, water, sanitation, housing, farming, and migration.
   - Key themes included integration of climate resilience into curricula, teacher training, and community engagement.

2. **Community Meetings**
   - Formal meetings were held with stakeholders at national, state, and local levels.
   - Three methods provided a platform for detailed discussions on policy, practice, and research.
   - Focused on the integration of climate resilience into educational policies.
   - Key themes included integration of climate resilience into curricula, teacher training, and community engagement.

3. **Workshops and Dialogues**
   - Workshops were held to facilitate more in-depth discussion on specific aspects of the BRIACE project.
   - Aimed at developing a shared understanding of the project goals and building collaboration among stakeholders.

### Outcomes of the Consultations

The consultations yielded valuable insights that will inform the BRIACE project's design and implementation:

- Identification of critical gaps in current educational practices related to climate change.
- Enhanced understanding of regional disparities and specific needs.
- Development of a shared vision for climate resilience policies.
- Identification of key-in and support for the project.
